The war chief looked well, and I have since heard was constantly among his soldiers, who were sick and dying, administering to their wants, and had not caught the disease from them and I thought it absurd to think that any of the people on the steamboat could be afraid of catching the disease from a well man. But these people are not brave like war chiefs, who never fear anything.

On our way down, I surveyed the country that had cost us so much trouble, anxiety and blood, and that now caused me to be a prisoner of war. I reflected upon the ingratitude of the whites when I saw their fine houses, rich harvests and everything desirable around them; and recollected that all this land had been ours, for which I and my people had never received a dollar, and that the whites were not satisfied until they took our village and our graveyards from us and removed us across the Mississippi.

===Chinese===
「白海狸」已顺河而下，代理人便把我移交克劳福德堡（Fort Crawford）的指挥官。我们在堡中稍事停留，便乘汽船押往杰斐逊兵营（Jefferson Barracks），负责押送的是位年轻的战首（杰斐逊·戴维斯中尉，Lieut. Jefferson Davis），他待我们十分宽厚。他是位善良而英勇的年轻首领，他的行事让我深为敬重。顺流而下途中，我们在加利纳停靠了片刻。人们涌到船边来看我们，那位战首却不许他们进我们所在的舱房——他将心比心，知道我们不愿被一群围观者盯着看。

我们路过罗克岛，没有停靠。当时在阿姆斯特朗堡的大战首斯科特将军（Gen. Scott）乘小船出来看我们，但汽船船长不许堡里任何人登船——军中霍乱正炽。我至今认为，船长本该准那位战首上船见我，我看不出这有什么可担心的。那位战首气色极好；后来我听说，他终日守在病亡的士兵中间照料，也不曾染疫。汽船上的人竟怕从一个康健之人身上过病气，我以为荒唐。只是这些人不像战首那般无畏——真正的战首，从不惧怕任何东西。

顺流而下的一路，我环视这片土地——它让我们付出了多少艰辛、忧惧与鲜血，如今又让我沦为战俘。望见他们华美的宅院、丰饶的收成和身边种种令人艳羡之物，我便想起白人的负义——这一切土地原本都是我们的，我与我族从未为此得过一块钱；而白人犹不满足，直到夺去我们的村落和墓地，把我们赶过密西西比河。

===Original===
On our arrival at Jefferson Barracks we met the great war chief, White Beaver, who had commanded the American army against my little band. I felt the humiliation of my situation; a little while before I had been leader of my braves, now I was a prisoner of war, but had surrendered myself. He received us kindly and treated us well.

We were now confined to the barracks and forced to wear the ball and chain. This was extremely mortifying and altogether useless. Was the White Beaver afraid I would break out of his barracks and run away? Or was he ordered to inflict this punishment upon me? If I had taken him prisoner on the field of battle I would not have wounded his feelings so much by such treatment, knowing that a brave war chief would prefer death to dishonor. But I do not blame the White Beaver for the course he pursued, as it is the custom among the white soldiers, and I suppose was a part of his duty.

===Chinese===
抵达杰斐逊兵营后，我们见到了大战首「白海狸」——正是他统率美军讨伐我这小小一队人马。我尝到了处境的屈辱：不久前我还是勇士们的首领，如今成了战俘——虽是自行投案的。他宽和地接纳了我们，待我们不薄。

我们被关进兵营，被迫戴上铁球镣链。这极端屈辱，也全然无用。难道「白海狸」怕我越营而逃？还是奉命加我以此惩罚？倘若战场上换作我擒了他，我决不会这样折辱他——我知道，英勇的战首宁死不受辱。不过我并不怪「白海狸」这么做——这是白人军队的惯例，我想也是他职责的一部分。

===Original===
The time dragged heavily and gloomily along throughout the winter, although the White Beaver did everything is his power to render us comfortable. Having been accustomed, throughout a long life, to roam the forests o’er, to go and come at liberty, confinement, and under such circumstances, could not be less than torture.

We passed away the time making pipes until spring, when we were visited by the agent, trader and interpreter, from Rock Island, Keokuk and several chiefs and braves of our nation, and my wife and daughter. I was rejoiced to see the two latter and spent my time very agreeably with them and my people as long as they remained.

The trader, Sagenash, (Col. Davenport) presented me with some dried venison, which had been killed and cured by some of my friends. This was a valuable present, and although he had given me many before, none ever pleased me so much. This was the first meat I had eaten for a long time that reminded me of the former pleasures of my own wigwam, which had always been stored with plenty.

===Chinese===
那个冬天，日子在沉重阴郁中煎熬——尽管「白海狸」已竭尽所能让我们过得舒适。我大半生都惯于在林海间自由来去、无拘无束；这般囚居，又落在如此境地，无异于酷刑。

我们靠雕制烟斗打发时光，直到春天——罗克岛的代理人、商人和通译来看望我们，同来的还有基奥卡克、我族的几位首领和勇士，以及我的妻子和女儿。见到妻女，我欣喜万分；他们逗留期间，我同她们和族人过得十分融洽。

商人萨格纳什（Sagenash，即达文波特上校）赠我一些干鹿肉，是我的几位朋友猎获腌制的。这份礼物贵重之至；他从前赠过我的东西不少，却从没有一样这样让我欢喜。很久以来，这是我吃到的第一口让我想起自家棚屋旧日富足滋味的肉——那时我家里的存粮向来是满的。

===Original===
Keokuk and his chiefs, during their stay at the barracks, petitioned our Great Father, the president, to release us, and pledged themselves for our good conduct. I now began to hope I would soon be restored to liberty and the enjoyment of my family and friends, having heard that Keokuk stood high in the estimation of our Great Father, because he did not join me in the war, but I was soon disappointed in my hopes. An order came from our Great Father to the White Beaver to send us on to Washington.

In a little while all were ready and left Jefferson Barracks on board of a steamboat, under charge of a young war chief and one soldier, whom the White Beaver sent along as a guide to Washington. We were accompanied by Keokuk, wife and son, Appanooce, Wapello, Poweshiek, Pashippaho, Nashashuk, Saukee, Musquaukee, and our interpreter. Our principal traders, Col. Geo. Davenport, of Rock Island, and S. S. Phelps and clerk, William Cousland, of the Yellow Banks, also accompanied us. On our way up the Ohio we passed several large villages, the names of which were explained to me. The first is called Louisville, and is a very petty village, situated on the bank of the Ohio River. The next is Cincinnati, which stands on the bank of the same river. This is a large and beautiful village and seemed to be in a thriving condition. The people gathered on the bank as we passed, in great crowds, apparently anxious to see us.

===Chinese===
基奥卡克和他的首领们在兵营盘留期间，向我们的父亲、总统呈请释放我们，并为我们日后的操行作保。听说基奥卡克因未曾随我开战而深得大父亲器重，我便开始指望很快就能重获自由，与家人亲友团聚；可这指望不久便落了空。大父亲传令「白海狸」：把我们解往华盛顿。

不多时一切齐备，我们登上一艘汽船离开杰斐逊兵营，由一位年轻战首押送，「白海狸」另派一名士兵随行充当去华盛顿的向导。随行同路的还有基奥卡克夫妇及其子、阿帕努斯（Appanooce）、瓦佩洛、波西克（Poweshiek）、帕希帕霍（Pashippaho）、纳沙舒克（Nashashuk）、索基（Saukee）、马斯夸基（Musquaukee）和我们的通译。我们的两位主要商人——罗克岛的乔治·达文波特上校、黄岸（Yellow Banks）的 S. S. 菲尔普斯（S. S. Phelps）及其文书威廉·库斯兰（William Cousland）——也同行。溯俄亥俄河（Ohio）而上，我们途经几座大村镇，名字都有人讲给我听。头一座叫路易斯维尔（Louisville），是座很小的村子，坐落在俄亥俄河边。下一座是辛辛那提（Cincinnati），也在同一条河边。这是座美丽的大村镇，一派兴旺气象。船过之时，人们成群聚在岸边，都急于一睹我们的样子。

===Original===
On our arrival at Wheeling the streets and river banks were crowded with people, who flocked from every direction to see us. While we remained here many called upon us and treated us with kindness, no one offering to molest or misuse us. This village is not so large as either of those before mentioned, but is quite a pretty one.

We left the steamboat then, having traveled a long distance on the prettiest river I ever saw (except our Mississippi) and took the stage. Being unaccustomed to this mode of traveling, we soon got tired and wished ourselves seated in a canoe on one of our own rivers, that we might return to our friends. We had traveled but a short distance before our carriage turned over, from which I received a slight injury, and the soldier had one arm broken. I was sorry for this accident, as the young man had behaved well.

We had a rough and mountainous country for several days, but had a good trail for our carriage. It is astonishing what labor and pains the white people have had to make this road, as it passes over several mountains, which are generally covered with rocks and timber, yet it has been made smooth and easy to travel upon.

===Chinese===
抵达惠灵（Wheeling）时，街上和河岸挤满了人，从四面八方赶来看我们。停留期间，许多人来探访，待我们和善，无人前来骚扰欺辱。这座镇子不及前面提到的那两座大，却相当秀丽。

随后我们下了汽船——在那条我所见过最美的河上（仅次于我们的密西西比河）已行了很远的路程——改乘驿马车。这种走法我们不惯，不多时便乏了，直想坐回自家河上的独木舟，好回到亲友身边。没走出多远，马车便翻了；我受了点轻伤，那士兵断了一条胳膊。出了这桩意外我心里难过——那年轻人素来行事端谨。

此后数日，我们穿行在崎岖的山地间，好在马车走的路道极佳。这条路翻越几座大山——山上遍是岩石林木——白人们为修它付出的辛劳血汗，实在惊人；竟把它修得平坦好走。

===Original===
Rough and mountainous as this country is there are many wigwams and small villages standing on the roadside. I could see nothing in the country to induce the people to live in it, and was astonished to find so many whites living on the hills.

I have often thought of them since my return to my own people, and am happy to think that they prefer living in their own country to coming out to ours and driving us from it, as many of the whites have already done. I think with them, that wherever the Great Spirit places his people they ought to be satisfied to remain, and be thankful for what He has given them, and not drive others from the country He has given them because it happens to be better then theirs. This is contrary to our way of thinking, and from my intercourse with the whites, I have learned that one great principle of their religion is “to do unto others as you wish them to do unto you.” Those people in the mountains seem to act upon this principle, but the settlers on our frontiers and on our lands seem never to think of it, if we are to judge by their actions.

The first village of importance that we came to, after leaving the mountains, is called Hagerstown. It is a large village to be so far from a river and is very pretty. The people appear to live well and enjoy themselves much.

===Chinese===
这地方如此山荒石险，路旁却立着许多房舍和小小村镇。我看不出这片土地有什么吸引人安家的地方，见山上有这么多白人居住，深感诧异。

回到族人身边后，我常想起他们；想到他们宁可守着自己的家乡过活，也不愿跑到我们的土地上来赶走我们——已有太多白人那么干了——我心里便觉宽慰。我与他们所见相同：大神把他的子民安放在哪里，他们就该在哪里安居知足，感念他所赐予的一切，不该因为别人的土地恰比自家的好，就把人家从大神赐予的土地上赶走。这原是与我们不同的想法。与白人交往下来，我得知他们宗教的一大原则是：「你们愿意人怎样待你们，你们也要怎样待人。」山中那些人似乎是奉行此道的；可我们边地和土地上的那些移民——若以行止而论——似乎从未想过这一条。

出山之后，我们途经的头一座像样的镇子叫黑格斯敦（Hagerstown）。离河这么远还能有这般规模，实属可观，镇子也相当漂亮。镇上人日子过得殷实，其乐融融。

===Original===
We passed through several small villages on the way to Fredericktown, but I have forgotten their names. This last is a large and beautiful village. The people treated us well, as they did at all other villages where we stopped.

Here we came to another road much more wonderful than that through the mountains. They call it a railroad, (the Baltimore and Ohio). I examined it carefully, but need not describe it, as the whites know all about it. It is the most astonishing sight I ever saw. The great road over the mountains will bear no comparison to it, although it has given the white people much trouble to make. I was surprised to see so much money and labor expended to make a good road for easy traveling. I prefer riding horseback, however, to any other way, but suppose these people would not have gone to so much trouble and expense to make a road if they did not prefer riding in their new fashioned carriages, which seem to run without any trouble, being propelled by steam on the same principle that boats are on the river. They certainly deserve great praise for their industry.

On our arrival at Washington, we called to see our Great Father, the President. He looks as if he had seen as many winters as I have, and seems to be a great brave. I had very little talk with him, as he appeared to be busy and did not seem to be much disposed to talk. I think he is a good man; and although he talked but little, he treated us very well. His wigwam is well furnished with everything good and pretty, and is very strongly built.

===Chinese===
去弗雷德里克敦（Fredericktown）的路上又穿过几座小镇，名字我忘了。弗雷德里克敦是座美丽的大镇。镇上人待我们极好——凡我们停留过的村镇，无不如是。

从这里，我们上了另一条比穿山公路神奇得多的路。他们管它叫铁路（巴尔的摩—俄亥俄铁路，Baltimore and Ohio）。我细细察看了一番，但无须描述——白人对它早已了如指掌。这是我这辈子见过最令人惊叹的景象。翻山的那条大路虽也教白人吃了不少苦头，与它相比却不值一提。见他们为修一条好走的路耗下这么多金钱与劳力，我着实惊讶。不过依我，出门还是骑马最好。想这些人大约是偏爱乘他们那新式车厢——那车似行走得毫不费力，同河上的船一样靠蒸汽推动——否则断不会这般费钱费力地修路。他们的勤劳，实在当得起盛赞。

抵达华盛顿后，我们去拜见了我们的父亲——总统。看面相，他过的冬天大约同我一样多了，像是一位大勇之人。同他交谈不多——他看着事务繁忙，不大有攀谈的兴致。我想他是个好人；话虽少，待我们极好。他的「大屋」陈设精美，一应俱全，造得也极坚固。

===Original===
He said he wished to know the cause of my going to war against his white children. I thought he ought to have known this before; and consequently said but little to him about it, as I expected he knew as well as I could tell him.

He said he wanted us to go to Fortress Monroe and stay awhile with the war chief who commanded it. But having been so long from my people, I told him that I would rather return to my nation; that Keokuk had come here once on a visit to him, as we had done, and he had let him return again, as soon as he wished, and that I expected to be treated in the same manner. He insisted, however, on our going to Fortress Monroe; and as the interpreter then present could not understand enough of our language to interpret a speech, I concluded it was best to obey our Great Father, and say nothing contrary to his wishes.

During our stay at the city, we were called upon by many of the people, who treated us well, particularly the squaws; we visited the great council home of the Americans; the place where they keep their big guns; and all the public buildings, and then started for Fortress Monroe. The war chief met us on our arrival, and shook hands, and appeared glad to see me. He treated us with great friendship, and talked to me frequently. Previous to our leaving this fort, he made us a feast, and gave us some presents, which I intend to keep for his sake. He is a very good man and a great brave. I was sorry to leave him, although I was going to return to my people, because he had treated me like a brother, during all the time I remained with him.

===Chinese===
他说想听听，我为何要同他的白人子民开战。我琢磨这他早该知道了；便没多说——料想他知道的，不会比我能讲给他的少。

他说想让我们去门罗要塞（Fortress Monroe），同驻守那里的战首盘桓些时日。可我离开族人已久，便对他说，我宁愿就此回归我族；基奥卡克从前也像我们这样来访过，他想回去，便随时放行；我指望自己也得同等的对待。他却执意要我们去门罗要塞；当时在场的通译又听不大懂我们的语言、译不了成篇的话，我想还是顺从大父亲为上，不违他的意。

在城里盘留期间，许多人来访，待我们甚好，妇人们尤其热忱；我们参观了美国人的议事大堂、存放大炮的地方和所有公共建筑，随后启程前往门罗要塞。抵达时，那位战首出迎，与我们握手，见到我似很高兴。他待我们情谊深厚，常与我交谈。临离要塞前，他为我们设宴，赠了些礼物——我打算好好保存，看在他的情分上。他是位极好的人，一位大勇之士。虽然就要回到族人身边，临别他仍让我难舍——同他相处的日子，他始终待我如手足。

===Original===
Having got a new guide, a war chief (Maj. Garland), we started for our own country, taking a circuitous route. Our Great Father being about to pay a visit to his children in the big towns towards sunrise, and being desirous that we should have an opportunity of seeing them, had directed our guide to take us through.

On our arrival at Baltimore, we were much astonished to see so large a village; but the war chief told us we would soon see a larger one. This surprised us more. During our stay here, we visited all the public buildings and places of amusement, saw much to admire, and were well entertained by the people who crowded to see us. Our Great Father was there at the same time, and seemed to be much liked by his white children, who flocked around him, (as they had around us) to shake him by the hand. He did not remain long, having left the city before us. In an interview, while here, the President said:

“When I saw you in Washington, I told you that you had behaved very badly in going to war against the whites. Your conduct then compelled me to send my warriors against you, and your people were defeated with great loss, and several of you surrendered, to be kept until I should be satisfied that you would not try to do any more injury. I told you, too, that I would inquire whether your people wished you to return, and whether, if you did return, there would be any danger to the frontier. Gen. Clark and Gen. Atkinson, whom you know, have informed me that your principal chief and the rest of your people are anxious you should return, and Keokuk has asked me to send you back. Your chiefs have pledged themselves for your good conduct, and I have given directions that you should be taken to your own country.

===Chinese===
我们换了新向导——一位战首（加兰少校，Maj. Garland）——取道迂回，踏上归乡之路。我们的大父亲将东行去日升方向的大城探望他的子民，有心让我们也开开眼界，便嘱咐向导带我们绕行经过。

到了巴尔的摩（Baltimore），这么大的镇子已让我们惊叹不已；战首却说，更大的还在后头。这话更让我们惊奇。盘留期间，我们参观了所有公共建筑和游乐场所，处处令人称羡；围观的人潮待我们殷勤周到。我们的大父亲那时也在城里，他的白人子民似乎很爱戴他，纷纷围拢上去同他握手——就像先前围拢我们那样。他没有久留，先我们一步离了城。在此地的一次会见中，总统说：

「在华盛顿见你们时，我便说过：你们对白人开战，行止大谬。你们此举逼得我派战士前去征讨；你们的人惨败，折损极重，你们数人投案，收押至今——直到我确信你们不再图谋为害。我还说过，要查明你们的人是否盼你们回去，以及你们回去之后，边地可有隐患。你们认识的克拉克将军（Gen. Clark）与阿特金森将军已向我禀报：你们的主要首领和其余族人都切盼你们回去，基奥卡克也已请求我送你们返乡。你们的首领已为你们的操行作保，我已下令送你们回自己的土地去。

===Original===
“Major Garland, who is with you, will conduct you through some of our towns. You will see the strength of the white people. You will see that our young men are as numerous as the leaves in the woods. What can you do against us? You may kill a few women and children, but such a force would seen be sent against you as would destroy your whole tribe. Let the red men hunt and take care of their families. I hope they will not again raise the tomahawk against their white brethren. We do not wish to injure you. We desire your prosperity and improvement. But if you again make war against our people, I shall send a force which will severely punish you. When you go back, listen to the councils of Keokuk and the other friendly chiefs; bury the tomahawk and live in peace with the people on the frontier. And I pray the Great Spirit to give you a smooth path and a fair sky to return.”

I was pleased with our Great Father’s talk and thanked him. Told him that the tomahawk had been buried so deep that it would never be resurrected, and that my remaining days would be spent in peace with all my white brethren.

We left Baltimore in a steamboat, and traveled in this way to the big village, where they make medals and money (Philadelphia). We again expressed surprise at finding this village so much larger than the one we had left; but the war chief again told us we would see another much larger than this. I had no idea that the white people had such large villages, and so many people. They were very kind to us, showed us all their great public works, their ships and steamboats. We visited the place where they make money, (the mint) and saw the men engaged at it. They presented each of us with a number of pieces of the coin as they fell from the mint, which are very handsome.

===Chinese===
「随行的加兰少校会带你们走访我们的一些城镇。你们将亲眼见到白人的实力。你们会看到，我们的年轻人多得像林中的树叶。你们能拿我们怎么样？你们或许能杀几个妇孺，但顷刻之间，足以毁灭你们整个部族的大军便会压到你们头上。让红种人安心打猎、照料家人吧。愿他们不再向白人兄弟举起战斧。我们无意伤害你们，只盼你们兴旺进步。但你们若再对我们的子民开战，我必派遣大军，严惩不贷。回去之后，要听从基奥卡克和其他友善首领的议事；埋掉战斧，与边地的人们和睦相处。我也祈求大神，赐你们归途一路坦荡、晴空万里。」

父亲这番话让我欣慰，我向他道了谢。我告诉他：战斧已埋得极深，永无重见天日之时；我的余年，定与所有白人兄弟和平共处。

我们乘汽船离开巴尔的摩，一路行到那座打造奖章和钱币的大镇（费城，Philadelphia）。这座镇子比刚离开的那座还大出许多，我们又惊又叹；战首却说，还有一座比这更大的等着我们。我全然想不到，白人竟有这般大的城镇、这么多的人。他们对我们极其友善，领我们看遍了宏伟的公共工程、大船和汽船。我们参观了他们铸钱的地方（造币厂），看工人们埋头操作。他们还赠给我们每人几枚刚出造币厂的硬币，枚枚精美。

===Original===
I witnessed a militia training in this city, in which were performed a number of singular military feats. The chiefs and men were all well dressed, and exhibited quite a warlike appearance. I think our system of military parade far better than that of the whites, but as I am now done going to war I will not describe it, or say anything more about war, or the preparations necessary for it.

We next started for New York, and on our arrival near the wharf, saw a large collection of people gathered at Castle Garden. We had seen many wonderful sights in our way﻿—large villages, the great national road over the mountains, the railroad, steam carriages, ships, steamboats, and many other things; but we were now about to witness a sight more surprising than any of these. We were told that a man was going up in the air in a balloon. We watched with anxiety to see if this could be true; and to our utter astonishment, saw him ascend in the air until the eye could no longer perceive him. Our people were all surprised and one of our young men asked the Prophet if he was going up to see the Great Spirit?

After the ascension of the balloon, we landed and got into a carriage to go to the house that had been provided for our reception. We had proceeded but a short distance before the street was so crowded that it was impossible for the carriage to pass. The war chief then directed the coachman to take another street, and stop at a different house from the one we had intended. On our arrival here we were waited upon by a number of gentlemen, who seemed much pleased to see us. We were furnished with good rooms, good provisions, and everything necessary for our comfort.

===Chinese===
在这座城里，我观摩了一场民兵操演，见识了不少新奇的军事技艺。军官和士兵个个衣着齐整，颇有一股肃杀之气。论操阵列仪，我以为我们的规矩远胜白人；只是我已封刀罢战，便不再细表，关于战争及其种种准备，也一概不提了。

随后我们启程去纽约，船近码头时，只见城堡花园（Castle Garden）聚着黑压压的人群。一路行来，奇景见了无数——大城镇、翻山的国道大路、铁路、蒸汽车厢、大船、汽船，不一而足；可即将目睹的这一幕，比先前一切都要惊人。有人告诉我们：有个人要乘气球上天。我们屏息定睛，看这事是真是假；只见他冉冉升空，直到肉眼再也望不见——我们全都惊呆了。我们的人都惊诧不已，一个年轻人问先知：那人是不是要上天去见大神？

气球升天后，我们登岸上车，前往为我们备下的接待居所。没走多远，街上人潮汹涌，马车寸步难行。战首只得吩咐车夫改走别街，停到另一处宅子。到那里后，许多绅士前来照应，见到我们似乎十分欢喜。房间舒适、饭食丰盛，安身所需一应俱全。

===Original===
The chiefs of this big village, being desirous that all their people should have an opportunity to see us, fitted up their great council home for this purpose, where we saw an immense number of people; all of whom treated us with great friendship, and many with great generosity. One of their great chiefs, John A. Graham, waited upon us and made a very pretty talk, which appeared in the village papers, one of which I now hand you.

> “**Brothers**: Open your ears. You are brave men. You have fought like tigers, but in a bad cause. We have conquered you. We were sorry last year that you raised the tomahawk against us; but we believe you did not know us then as you do now. We think, in time to come, you will be wise, and that we shall be friends forever. You see that we are a great people, numerous as the flowers of the field, as the shells on the sea shore, or the fishes in the sea. We put one hand on the eastern, and at the same time the other on the western ocean. We all act together. If some time our great men talk long and loud at our council fires, but shed one drop of white men’s blood, our young warriors, as thick as the stars of the night, will leap aboard of our great boats, which fly on the waves and over the lakes﻿—swift as the eagle in the air﻿—then penetrate the woods, make the big guns thunder, and the whole heavens red with the flames of the dwellings of their enemies. Brothers, the President has made you a great talk. He has but one mouth. That one has sounded the sentiments of all the people. Listen to what he has said to you. Write it on your memories, it is good, very good.

> “Black Hawk, take these jewels, a pair of topaz earrings, beautifully set in gold, for your wife or daughter, as a token of friendship, keeping always in mind, that women and children are the favorites of the Great Spirit. These jewels are from an old man, whose head is whitened with the snows of seventy winters, an old man who has thrown down his bow, put off his sword, and now stands leaning on his staff, waiting the commands of the Great Spirit. Look around you, see all this mighty people, then go to your homes, open your arms to receive your families. Tell them to bury the hatchet, to make bright the chain of friendship, to love the white men, and to live in peace with them, as long as the rivers run into the sea, and the sun rises and sets. If you do so, you will be happy. You will then ensure the prosperity of unborn generations of your tribes, who will go hand in hand with the sons of the white men, and all shall be blessed by the Great Spirit. Peace and happiness by the blessing of the Great Spirit attend you. Farewell.”

===Chinese===
这座大镇的酋长们有意让全镇人都见上我们一面，特地把他们宏大的议事堂腾出来供此用。我们在那里见到了人山人海，人人待我们情深谊厚，许多人出手极为慷慨。他们的一位大首领约翰·A·格雷厄姆（John A. Graham）亲来致意，发表了一篇极漂亮的演说，刊登在镇上的报章上，其中一份，我现在交给你。

> 「**兄弟们**：竖起你们的耳朵。你们是勇士，打得像猛虎，只可惜师出无名。我们征服了你们。去年你们对我们举起战斧，我们引以为憾；但我们相信，那时你们还不认识我们，如今你们认识了。我们想，往后你们会明智起来，我们将永远是朋友。你们看到了，我们是一个伟大的民族，人数之多，如原野的花、海滩的贝、海中的鱼。我们一只手按着东边的海洋，另一只手同时按着西边的海洋。我们万众一心。倘若哪天我们的大人物在议事火堆边说了重话，流了白人的一滴血，我们的年轻战士便会多如夜星，跃上我们劈波踏浪的巨船——迅捷如苍鹰掠空——深入你们的林地，教大炮轰鸣，让敌人居所的烈焰染红整个天穹。兄弟们，总统已对你们作了一番宏论。他只有一张口，而这一张口，道出了全体人民的心声。听他对你们说的话，把它铭刻在心——那是金玉良言，良言中的良言。

> 「黑鹰，请收下这对饰物——一对镶嵌在金座上的精美托帕石耳环，赠给你的妻子或女儿，作为友谊的信物；并请永远记住：妇孺是大神的至爱。这对饰物出自一位老人之手——他的头颅已被七十个冬天的霜雪染白；他放下了弓，卸下了剑，如今拄杖而立，静候大神的召唤。环顾你的四周，看看这强大的人民，然后回到你的家园，张开双臂拥抱你的家人。告诉他们：埋掉战斧，擦亮友谊之链，爱护白人，与他们永享和平——只要江河仍归大海，太阳仍起仍落。如此，你们便得安乐。你们还将为部族未出世的世代奠定兴旺，他们将同白人的儿孙携手同行，同受大神的祝福。愿大神的祝福常伴你们，安乐相随。别了。」

===Original===
In reply to this fine talk, I said, “Brother: We like your talk. We like the white people. They are very kind to us. We shall not forget it. Your council is good. We shall attend to it. Your valuable present shall go to my squaw. We shall always be friends.”

The chiefs were particular in showing us everything that they thought would be pleasing or gratifying to us. We went with them to Castle Garden to see the fireworks, which was quite an agreeable entertainment, but to the whites who witnessed it, less magnificent than would have been the sight of one of our large prairies when on fire.

We visited all the public buildings and places of amusement, which, to us, were truly astonishing yet very gratifying.

===Chinese===
对这篇漂亮的演说，我答道：「兄弟：你的话我们爱听。我们喜爱白人。他们待我们极好，我们不会忘记。你的忠告是善的，我们必谨记遵行。你珍贵的礼物，我会交给我的妻子。我们永远是朋友。」

首领们格外用心，凡他们认为能让我们欢喜开眼的，一样不落，都带我们去看。我们随他们到城堡花园观焰火——那确是赏心乐事；不过在旁观的白人看来，比起我们大草原烈火燎原的景象，怕还要逊色几分。

我们参观了所有的公共建筑和游乐场所——于我们而言，桩桩件件匪夷所思，又处处令人欣喜。

===Original===
Everybody treated us with friendship, and many with great liberality. The squaws presented us many handsome little presents that are said to be valuable. They were very kind, very good, and very pretty﻿—for palefaces.

Among the men, who treated us with marked friendship, by the presentation of many valuable presents, I cannot omit to mention the name of my old friend Crooks, of the American Fur Company. I have known him long, and have always found him to be a good chief, one who gives good advice, and treats our people right. I shall always be proud to recognize him as a friend, and glad to shake him by the hand.

Being anxious to return to our people, our guide started with us for our own country. On arriving at Albany, the people were so anxious to see us, that they crowded the streets and wharfs, where the steamboats landed, so much, that it was almost impossible for us to pass to the hotel which had been provided for our reception. We remained here but a short time, it being a comparatively small village, with only a few large public buildings. The great council home of the state is located here, and the big chief (the governor) resides here, in an old mansion. From here we went to Buffalo, thence to Detroit, where I had spent many pleasant days, and anticipated, on my arrival, to meet many of my old friends, but in this I was disappointed. What could be the cause of this? Are they all dead? Or what has become of them? I did not see our old father there, who had always given me good advice and treated me with great friendship.

===Chinese===
人人都待我们以友谊，许多人慷慨大方。妇人们赠了我们许多精致的小礼物，据说颇为贵重。她们和善极了，好极了，也美极了——以青白面孔的人而论。

在以重礼相赠、待我们情谊笃厚的男士中，我不能不提老友克鲁克斯（Crooks）的大名，他隶属美国皮货公司。我与他相识已久，始终觉得他是位好首领——献良言，待我族公正。认他做朋友，我将永远引以为荣；能与他握手，我由衷欢喜。

归心似箭，向导便带着我们启程返乡。到奥尔巴尼时，人们争睹心切，街道和汽船码头挤得水泄不通，我们几乎挤不到备好的接待旅店去。我们在此只稍作停留——这是座相对小的镇子，只有寥寥几座大型公共建筑。本州的议事大堂设在镇上，大首领（州长）也住在这里，一座古老的宅邸里。由此我们去布法罗（Buffalo），再到底特律——我曾在那里度过许多愉快的日子，满以为此番抵达必能重逢诸多旧友，谁知竟落了空。这是何故？他们都不在世了吗？还是都去了哪里？我没能在那里见到我们的老父亲——他向来给我善言，待我情谊深厚。

===Original===
After leaving Detroit it was but a few days before we landed at Prairie du Chien. The war chief at the fort treated us very kindly, as did the people generally. I called on the agent of the Winnebagoes, (Gen. J. M. Street), to whom I had surrendered myself after the battle at Bad Axe, who received me very friendly. I told him that I had left my great medicine bag with his chiefs before I gave myself up; and now, that I was to enjoy my liberty again, I was anxious to get it, that I might head it down to my nation unsullied.

He said it was safe; he had heard his chiefs speak of it, and would get it and send it to me. I hope he will not forget his promise, as the whites generally do, because I have always heard that he was a good man, and a good father, and made no promise that he did not fulfill.

Passing down the Mississippi, I discovered a large collection of people in the mining country, on the west side of the river, and on the ground that we had given to our relation, Dubuque, a long time ago. I was surprised at this, as I had understood from our Great Father that the Mississippi was to be the dividing line between his red and white children, and he did not wish either to cross it. I was much pleased with this talk, and I knew it would be much better for both parties. I have since found the country much settled by the whites further down, and near to our people, on the west side of the river. I am very much afraid that in a few years they will begin to drive and abuse our people, as they have formerly done. I may not live to see it, but I feel certain the day is not far distant.

===Chinese===
离开底特律后不过几天，我们便在普雷里迪欣登岸。堡中的战首待我们极为亲切，当地人也大都如此。我去拜望了温尼贝戈人的代理人（J. M. 斯特里特将军，Gen. J. M. Street）——巴德阿克斯（Bad Axe）之战后，我便是向他投降的；他热诚地接待了我。我告诉他：投降之前，我把大药囊留在了他的首领们那里；如今我即将重获自由，急欲取回它，好把它清清白白地传给我的部族。

他说药囊安然无恙；他听首领们提起过，必取来送还给我。我盼他莫像寻常白人那样忘了承诺——我一向听说他是好人、好父亲，从不许空愿。

沿密西西比河而下，我看见河西岸矿区聚起了大群的人——那片土地，我们很久以前给了我们的亲戚迪比克（Dubuque）。这让我诧异：我曾听大父亲亲口说，密西西比河是他红白两种子民的分界，哪一边他都不要越界。这话我当时听了很是欢喜，也知道若能践行，于两造都是大好事。此后我发现，再往下游，河西岸靠近我族的地方，白人已垦殖甚众。我深恐不出几年，他们便会故态复萌，驱逐欺凌我们的族人。我未必活得见到那一天，但我确知，那一天已不远了。

===Original===
When we arrived at Rock Island, Keokuk and the other chiefs were sent for. They arrived the next day with a great number of their young men, and came over to see me. I was pleased to see them, and they all appeared glad to see me. Among them were some who had lost relations the year before. When we met, I perceived the tear of sorrow gush from their eyes at the recollection of their loss, yet they exhibited a smiling countenance, from the joy they felt at seeing me alive and well.

The next morning, the war chief, our guide, convened a council at Fort Armstrong. Keokuk and his party went to the fort; but, in consequence of the war chief not having called for me to accompany him, I concluded that I would wait until I was sent for. Consequently, the interpreter came and said, “they were ready, and had been waiting for me to come to the fort.” I told him I was ready and would accompany him. On our arrival there the council commenced. The war chief said that the object of this council was to deliver me up to Keokuk. He then read a paper, and directed me to follow Keokuk’s advice, and be governed by his counsel in all things! In this speech he said much that was mortifying to my feelings, and I made an indignant reply.

===Chinese===
我们抵达罗克岛后，有人去请基奥卡克和其他首领。次日，他们带着大队年轻人赶到，过来看我。见到他们我很欢喜，他们见了我，也个个面露欣色。其中有些人，前一年折了亲人。相见之时，我看得分明：忆及亡亲，悲泪夺眶而出；可又因见我生还无恙，脸上漾着笑意。

次日清晨，我们的向导、那位战首，在阿姆斯特朗堡召集议事会。基奥卡克一行去了堡中；战首没有唤我同行，我便决意等他们来请。果然，通译来传话：「人都齐了，正等我去堡中。」我说我这就去，随他动身。一到堡中，议事便开始了。战首宣布：此番议事，为的是把我交托给基奥卡克。接着他宣读了一份文书，吩咐我事事听从基奥卡克的指点、受他的裁夺！他这番话里许多地方深深刺痛了我，我作了愤然的答复。

===Original===
I do not know what object the war chief had in making such a speech; or whether he intended what he said; but I do know that it was uncalled for, and did not become him. I have addressed many war chiefs and listened to their speeches with pleasure, but never had my feelings of pride and honor insulted on any other occasion. But I am sorry I was so hasty in reply to this chief, because I said that which I did not intend.

In this council I met my old friend (Col. Wm. Davenport,) whom I had known about eighteen years. He is a good and brave chief. He always treated me well, and gave me good advice. He made me a speech on this occasion, very different from that of the other chief. It sounded like coming from a brave. He said he had known me a long time, that we had been good friends during that acquaintance, and, although he had fought against my braves, in our late war, he still extended the hand of friendship to me, and hoped that I was now satisfied, from what I had seen in my travels, that it was folly to think of going to war against the whites, and would ever remain at peace. He said he would be glad to see me at all times, and on all occasions would be happy to give me good advice.

If our Great Father were to make such men our agents he would much better subserve the interests of our people, as well as his own, than in any other way. The war chiefs all know our people, and are respected by them. If the war chiefs at the different military posts on the frontier were made agents, they could always prevent difficulties from arising among the Indians and whites; and I have no doubt, had the war chief above alluded to been our agent, we would never have had the difficulties with the whites we have had. Our agents ought always to be braves. I would, therefore, recommend to our Great Father the propriety of breaking up the present Indian establishment, and creating a new one, and make the commanding officers at the different frontier posts the agents of the Government for the different nations of Indians.

===Chinese===
那位战首发表这样一番话，用意何在，我不明白；他说的可是真心话，我也不知道；我只知道：那些话全无必要，也有失他的身份。我曾与许多战首对谈，欢欢喜喜听过他们的演说；可我的自尊与荣誉感从未在其他任何场合受过这样的凌辱。只是我懊悔当时答他答得太急——我说了些本不想说的话。

这次议事会上，我遇见了老友（威廉·达文波特上校，Col. Wm. Davenport）——相识已有十八年左右。他是位善良而英勇的首领，素来待我不薄，多有善言。这一回他也对我致辞，与另一位首领的言辞大异其趣——那才像出自一位勇士之口。他说他与我相识已久，相交以来一直是好朋友；虽说在刚刚过去的那场战争里，他曾与我的勇士们兵戎相见，如今他仍向我伸出友谊之手；并望我此番游历所见，已叫我认清对白人开战何其愚妄，从此永享太平。他还说，无论何时相见他都欢喜，凡有大事，必乐于赐我良言。

倘若大父亲派这样的人做我们的代理人，无论于我族还是于他自己，都是最两利的安排。战首们都熟识我们族人，也深受族人的敬重。若让边地各军事哨所的战首兼任代理之职，印第安人与白人之间的纠纷随时都可消弭于未萌。我毫不怀疑：倘方才说到的那位战首早是我们的代理人，我们与白人之间那场祸事根本不会发生。我们的代理人，理应永远是勇士。因此我愿向大父亲进言：不妨撤销如今的印第安事务旧制，另立新章，让各边地哨所的指挥官出任政府派驻各印第安部族的代理人。

===Original===
I have a good opinion of the American war chiefs generally with whom I am acquainted, and my people, who had an opportunity of seeing and becoming well acquainted with the great war chief (Gen. Winfield Scott), who made the last treaty with them, in conjunction with the great chief of Illinois (Governor Reynolds), all tell me that he is the greatest brave they ever saw, and a good man﻿—one who fulfills his promises. Our braves spoke more highly of him than of any chief that had ever been among us, or made treaties with us. Whatever he says may be depended upon. If he had been our Great Father we never would have been compelled to join the British in the last war with America, and I have thought that as our Great Father is changed every few years, that his children would do well to put this great war chief in his place, for they cannot find a better chief for a Great Father anywhere.

I would be glad if the village criers (editors), in all the villages I passed through, would let their people know my wishes and opinions about this great war chief.

During my travels my opinions were asked for on different subjects, but for want of a good interpreter (our regular interpreter having gone home on a different route), were seldom given. Presuming that they would be equally acceptable now, I have thought it a part of my duty to lay the most important before the public.

===Chinese===
我所相识的美国战首们，大抵印象都好。我的族人曾有机会亲眼见识、并深为熟稔那位大战首（温菲尔德·斯科特将军，Gen. Winfield Scott）——他与伊利诺伊的大首领（雷诺兹州长，Governor Reynolds）共同与我族订立了最后的条约——族人们众口一词告诉我：那是他们平生所见最伟大的勇士，也是个好人——言出必行。我们的勇士们对他的称许，超过了对任何来过我们中间、与我们缔过约的首领。他的话，句句靠得住。他若早是我们的父亲，上一次美英交战，我们绝不会被迫投奔英国人。我又想，大父亲之位每隔几年便换一人，他的子民不如把这位大战首扶上那个位置——要找一位更好的「父亲」首领，天下也寻不出了。

我一路经过的那些村镇的「传令人」（报人，editors），若肯把我对这位大战首的心意与看法转告他们的人民，我将十分欢喜。

游历途中，常有人就种种题目垂询我的看法；只可惜缺一位好通译（我们的随行通译已取道别路先行回家），多半未能奉答。想必这些见解如今也同样受欢迎，我便觉得有责任把其中最紧要的公之于众。

===Original===
The subject of colonizing the negroes was introduced and my opinion asked as to the best method of getting clear of these people. I was not fully prepared at that time to answer, as I knew but little about their situation. I have since made many inquiries on the subject, and find that a number of States admit no slaves, whilst the balance hold these negroes as slaves, and are anxious, but do not know how to get clear of them. I will now give my plan, which, when understood, I hope will be adopted.

Let the free States remove all the male negroes within their limits to the slave States; then let our Great Father buy all the female negroes in the slave States between the ages of twelve and twenty, and sell them to the people of the free States, for a term of years, say those under fifteen until they are twenty-one, and those of and over fifteen, for five years, and continue to buy all the females in the slave States as soon as they arrive at the age of twelve, and take them to the free States and dispose of them in the same way as the first, and it will not be long before the country is clear of the black-skins, about which I am told they have been talking for a long time, and for which they have expended a large amount of money.

I have no doubt but our Great Father would willingly do his part in accomplishing this object for his children, as he could not lose much by it, and would make them all happy. If the free States did not want them all for servants, we would take the balance in our nation to help our women make corn.

===Chinese===
有人曾向我提起向海外移置黑人的议题，并问我对「如何摆脱这些人」有何良策。我当时未及详答——他们的境况，我知之甚少。此后我就此事多方打听，方知有若干州不蓄奴，其余诸州则蓄这些黑人为奴，虽急欲脱手，却不知路在何方。现在我把我的方略献上，但愿世人明晓之后，能够采纳。

让自由州把境内全部黑人男子迁往蓄奴州；再让大父亲把蓄奴州中十二至二十岁的黑人女子尽数买下，以年为期转售给自由州的人民——十五岁以下者服劳至二十一岁，十五岁及以上者以五年为期——此后蓄奴州的黑人女子一到十二岁便照此收购，迁往自由州依前法处置。要不了多久，这个国家便再见不着黑皮肤的人了——听说为此他们已议论多年，也掷下了大把金钱。

我毫不怀疑，大父亲为了子民必乐于玉成此事——他破费有限，却可教他们皆大欢喜。自由州若用不了那么多仆役，余下的我族愿意收下，帮我们的妇人种玉米。

===Original===
I have not time now, or is it necessary to enter more into detail about my travels through the United States. The white people know all about them, and my people have started to their hunting grounds and I am anxious to follow them.

Before I take leave of the public, I must contradict the story of some of the village criers, who, I have been told, accuse me of having murdered women and children among the whites. This assertion is false! I never did, nor have I any knowledge that any of my nation ever killed a white woman or child. I make this statement of truth to satisfy the white people among whom I have been traveling, and by whom I have been treated with great kindness, that, when they shook me by the hand so cordially, they did not shake the hand that had ever been raised against any but warriors.

It has always been our custom to receive all strangers that come to our village or camps in time of peace on terms of friendship, to share with them the best provisions we have, and give them all the assistance in our power. If on a journey or lost, to put them on the right trail, and if in want of moccasins, to supply them. I feel grateful to the whites for the kind manner they treated me and my party whilst traveling among them, and from my heart I assure them that the white man will always be welcome in our village or camps, as a brother. The tomahawk is buried forever! We will forget what has passed, and may the watchword between the Americans and the Sacs and Foxes ever be﻿—**friendship**.

===Chinese===
至于我在美国游历的种种细节，如今既无闲暇细述，也无必要细述。白人早已知之甚详；我族的人已动身前往猎场，我巴不得即刻随他们同去。

向公众告辞之前，我必须驳斥某些报人的传言——听说他们指控我杀害过白人的妇孺。此说纯属捏造！我从未做过这种事；也毫不知晓我族有谁杀过白人的妇人或孩子。我讲出这句实话，是为让一路善待我的白人安心：他们那样热忱握过的这只手，从未举起对付过任何人——除了战士。

和平年月，凡有生人造访我们的村落营地，我们向来以友相待，拿出最好的吃食分享，倾力相助——这本是我们的老规矩。远行者迷了路，我们为他指正途；缺了鹿皮鞋，我们为他置办。游历期间他们待我和我的同伴如此仁厚，我心怀感激；我在此发自肺腑地保证：白人将永远受欢迎走进我们的村落营地，如兄弟一般。战斧已永远埋葬！过往种种，我们将尽数忘却；愿美国人与索克族、福克斯族之间永存的口令只有两个字——**友谊**。

===Original===
I am done now. A few more moons and I must follow my fathers to the shades. May the Great Spirit keep our people and the whites always at peace, is the sincere wish of

Black Hawk

===Chinese===
我的话讲完了。再过几个月圆，我便要随先人们去往幽冥。愿大神佑我族与白人永享和平——此乃一片至诚之心愿，出自

黑鹰（Black Hawk）

### The Black Hawk War / 黑鹰战争

===Original===
About the first of April Black Hawk’s band assembled at Fort Madison for the purpose of making arrangements to ascend the Mississippi, and soon after the entire party started. The old men, women and children, with their provisions and camp equipage, in canoes, and the men all armed, came on horseback. On the sixth day of April, the braves, on horseback, made a call at Yellow Banks, one day after the canoes had passed the same point, and told Josiah Smart, Mr. Phelps’ interpreter, where they were going, and the object of their visit. They said they had observed a great war chief, with a number of troops going up on a steamboat, and thought it likely that the mission of this war chief was to prevent them going up Rock river, but they were bound to go. Messrs. Phelps and Smart tried to persuade them to recross the river and return to their country, assuring them that the Government would not permit them to come into Illinois in violation of the treaty they had made last year, in which they had agreed to remain on the west side of the river. But they would not listen to their advice. On the next day they took up the line of march for Rock river, and on the 10th of April, 1832, Black Hawk, with a portion of his band of Sacs, reached the mouth of Rock river a few miles below Rock Island. The old men, women and children with their provisions and camp equipage, who came up in canoes, arrived on the 9th, and the men all armed, came up on horseback, reaching the camp on the 10th. While encamped there they were joined by the Prophet, who had previously invited them to come up to the country of the Winnebagoes and raise a crop. He called on his way at Fort Armstrong and had talks with the Agent and Col. Davenport, the trader, both of whom advised him to persuade Black Hawk and party to return to their own country, or they would be driven back by the soldiers then at Fort Armstrong, under the command of Gen. Atkinson, who had just arrived. The Prophet would not listen to their advice, but assured Black Hawk that he had a right to go forward with his entire party to the Winnebago country; and as he expected large reinforcements to his little army as he ascended Rock river, he was determined to go forward, but had given positive orders to his band, under no circumstances, to strike a blow until they had been reinforced by warriors from the Winnebagoes and Pottowattomies.

Early next morning they broke camp and started up Rock river, but were soon overtaken by a small detachment of soldiers, who held a council with Black Hawk and communicated to him the orders of Gen. Atkinson. These were for him to return with his band and re-cross the Mississippi. Black Hawk said, as he was not on the war path, but going on a friendly visit to the Prophet’s village, he intended to go forward, and continued on his journey. On receipt of his answer, Gen. Atkinson sent another detachment to Black Hawk with imperative orders for him to return, or he would pursue him with his entire army and drive him back. In reply, Black Hawk said the General had no right to make the order so long as his band was peaceable, and that he intended to go on to the Prophet’s village.

In the meantime the forces under the command of Gen. Whitesides had arrived, and were turned over to Gen. Atkinson by the Governor. The brigade, under the command of Gen. Whitesides, was ordered up Rock river to Dixon’s Ferry, and as soon as boats could be got ready, Gen. Atkinson started for the same destination with 300 regulars and about the same number of Illinois militia. Black Hawk with his party had already reached a point some thirty or forty miles above Dixon’s Ferry, where they were met in council by some Pottowattomies and Winnebago chiefs. They assured Black Hawk that their people would not join him in making war against the United States, and denied the Prophet’s story to him. During this council Black Hawk became convinced that he had been badly imposed upon by the Prophet, and resolved at once to send a flag of truce to Gen. Atkinson and ask permission to descend Rock river, re-cross the Mississippi and go back to their country.

===Chinese===
四月初，黑鹰的部众在麦迪逊堡集结，做出溯密西西比河而上的各项安排，不久全队便启程了。老人、妇女和儿童带着口粮和营帐行装乘独木舟而行，男人们则全副武装，骑马赶路。4 月 6 日，骑马的勇士们在黄岸作短暂停留——比独木舟队经过同一地点晚一天——并把此行的去向和目的告诉了菲尔普斯先生（Mr. Phelps）的译员乔赛亚·斯马特（Josiah Smart）。他们说，曾见一位大战酋带着一批部队乘汽船上行，猜想这位战酋的使命多半是阻拦他们溯罗克河而上，但他们非去不可。菲尔普斯与斯马特两位先生力劝他们折返渡河、回归自己的家园，并向他们保证：政府绝不会容许他们违反去年缔结的条约进入伊利诺伊州——条约中他们已允诺留在河的西岸。但他们不听劝告。次日，他们便向罗克河进发；1832 年 4 月 10 日，黑鹰率索克族部分部众抵达罗克岛下游数英里处的罗克河口。乘独木舟载着口粮和营帐行装上行的老人、妇女和儿童于 9 日到达；全副武装骑马而来的男人们于 10 日抵达营地。在彼处扎营期间，先知（the Prophet）前来与他们会合——正是他先前邀他们北上温尼贝戈人的地界种一季庄稼。他途中在阿姆斯特朗堡停留，与印第安事务官和商人达文波特上校会谈；两人都劝他说服黑鹰一行返回自己的家园，否则他们必被阿姆斯特朗堡的驻军驱逐回去——驻军刚由新到任的阿特金森将军统率。先知不听他们的劝告，反而向黑鹰保证：他有权率全队继续前往温尼贝戈人的地界；他料想沿罗克河而上时，他那支小小的队伍必会得到大批生力军，于是决意前进；但他已向部众下达死命令：无论如何，在温尼贝戈族和波塔瓦托米族的战士前来增援之前，绝不先动手。

次日清晨，他们拔营启程，溯罗克河而上，但很快便被一小队士兵追上；士兵们与黑鹰会谈，向他传达了阿特金森将军的命令：命他率部返回，重渡密西西比河。黑鹰说，他此来并非走上征伐之路，而是去先知的村子作友好访问，故仍要前行，说罢继续赶路。阿特金森将军得到这一答复后，又派出另一队士兵传令，措辞严厉：即刻返回，否则他将亲率全军追击，把他们赶回去。黑鹰答复说，只要他的部众安分无扰，将军便无权下这道命令，而且他决意继续前往先知的村子。

与此同时，怀特塞兹将军（Gen. Whitesides）麾下的部队已经开到，由州长拨归阿特金森将军节制。怀特塞兹将军的旅奉命沿罗克河上开迪克森渡口（Dixon’s Ferry）；船只一经备齐，阿特金森将军也率 300 名正规军和数目相当的伊利诺伊州民兵开赴同一目的地。其时黑鹰一行已抵达迪克森渡口上游约三四十英里处，在那里与几位波塔瓦托米族和温尼贝戈族酋长会商。他们向黑鹰言明，族人不会与他联手对合众国开战，并当面戳穿了先知的说法。这次会商使黑鹰确信自己受了先知的大骗，当即决意向阿特金森将军递出休战旗，请求准许他们顺罗克河而下、重渡密西西比河、返回自己的家园。

#### Stillman’s Defeat / 斯蒂尔曼之败

===Original===
About this time, Gen. Whitesides had concentrated a large force of militia at Dixon’s Ferry, and at the solicitation of Major Stillman, permitted him to take out a scouting party of nearly 300 mounted men. They went up Rock river, about thirty miles to Sycamore creek, and encamped within a few miles of Black Hawk’s camp, but were not aware of its position at the time. Indian scouts having intercepted their coming reported at once to Black Hawk that a large army of mounted militia were coming towards his camp; and before the volunteers had entirely completed their arrangements for encampment, outside guards espied three Indians coming in with a white flag. After holding a parley with them, (one of the guards being able to talk a little with them in their own language), they were hurried into camp, and before any explanations were made, the flag bearer was shot and instantly killed, whilst his comrades made their escape during the confusion in getting the regiment ready to pursue the fleeing Indians. These had secreted themselves in ambush as the army rushed by, helter skelter, after another small party of Indians who had followed the flag bearers, and who, when hearing the uproar in camp made a hasty retreat. The entire regiment was soon mounted and started out in squads towards the camp of Black Hawk. The latter having learned by a scout that the army was coming, started at once with less than fifty mounted warriors, his entire force then in camp, to meet the enemy, and on arriving at a copse of timber and underbrush near Sycamore creek, made ready to meet them.

Capt. Eads’ company, who were the first to start out, killed two of the five fleeing Indians. Soon after crossing Sycamore creek they were surprised by a terrific war whoop from the Indians, who were concealed in the bushes near by, and with deadly aim commenced firing into the front ranks of the regiment, and with unearthly yells (as one of the fleeing party told us on arriving at Galena), charged upon our ranks, with tomahawks raised, ready to slaughter all who might come within their reach. Judging from the yelling of the Indians, their number was variously estimated at from one thousand to two thousand.

The entire party was thrown into such confusion that Major Stillman had no control of any of them, and, with one exception, the entire army continued their flight to Dixon’s Ferry, thirty miles distant, whilst some went back to their homes.

The retreating army passed through their camping ground near Sycamore creek, where they should have halted, and under cover of the timber, could have shot down their pursuers while yet in open prairie. Black Hawk and a small portion of his command gave up the chase, and returned to his camp, while the remainder pursued the fugitives for several miles, occasionally overtaking and killing some soldiers, whose horses had given out.

===Chinese===
约在此时，怀特塞兹将军已在迪克森渡口集结起大批民兵；应斯蒂尔曼少校（Major Stillman）之请，准他率领近 300 名骑马志愿兵组成一支侦察队出发。他们沿罗克河上行约三十英里，抵达西卡莫尔溪（Sycamore creek），在距黑鹰营地不出数英里处扎营，当时并未察觉营地的位置。印第安探子探得他们的行踪，立即报知黑鹰：一支大队骑马民兵正朝他的营地开来。志愿兵们尚未把营地安顿停当，外围哨兵便望见三名印第安人执白旗前来。双方交涉一阵（其中一名哨兵尚能用印第安语同他们略作交谈）之后，来者被匆匆带入营地；一句解释都未及说，执旗者便被枪杀，当场毙命；他的两名同伴趁全团集结追击印第安溃兵的混乱之际脱身而走。原来另有一小队印第安人跟在执旗者身后，听见营中喧嚣，急忙后撤；这队人眼看大军乱作一团、蜂拥追出，便悄然隐伏于埋伏之处。全团随即上马，分作小队朝黑鹰的营地进发。黑鹰经探子得知大军将至，立即带上营中全部人马——不足五十名骑马战士——前去迎敌；行至西卡莫尔溪附近一片树木与灌木丛生之地，便整装待战。

伊兹上尉（Capt. Eads）的连队率先出动，击毙了五名溃逃印第安人中的两名。他们刚渡过西卡莫尔溪，便听得印第安人一声骇人的战吼——原来他们埋伏在近旁的灌木丛中——随即以致命的准头朝该团的前排开火，又发出声声非人世所有的呐喊（这是一名溃逃者逃抵加利纳后告诉我们的），高举战斧向我军队列冲杀而来，凡落入他们手边的都难逃屠戮。单凭呐喊声判断，印第安人的数目众说纷纭，从一千人到两千人不等。

全军乱作一团，斯蒂尔曼少校对部下完全失去了控制；除一人外，全军一路溃逃到三十英里外的迪克森渡口，有些人干脆逃回了家中。

溃军途经西卡莫尔溪附近的旧营地——本该在此据守：有林木掩护，本可在追兵尚在开阔草原上时将其一一射倒。黑鹰与其部众中的一小部分停止了追击，返回营地；其余的人则尾追溃兵数英里，不时追上马匹力竭的士兵，将其杀死。

===Original===
Among the retreating party was a Methodist preacher, whose horse was too slow to keep out of the reach of the Indians, who adopted a novel plan to save himself and horse. On coming to a ravine he left the track of his pursuers some distance, and followed down the ravine until he found a place deep enough to shelter himself and horse from view, and remained there for two hours in safety. He had the precaution to keep a strict count of the Indians as they went forward, and waited their return. Being satisfied that all had returned and continued on the way to their camp, he quietly left his hiding place, trotted leisurely along and reached Dixon’s Ferry about sunrise next morning.

He reported his mode of procedure and the strategy used to render his safety certain from the Indians who had dispersed and driven the army before them. He was interrogated into the number, and when he reported **twenty-five**, great indignation was manifested by some of the *brave* volunteers who had got into camp some hours before him, and reported the number at fifteen hundred to two thousand! But as he was well known to many of the volunteers and highly respected as a meek and lowly Christian gentleman, they stood by him and prevented any personal violence.

When the report of this fiasco came into Galena the next morning about 8 o’clock, on the 15th of May, our regiment was immediately called to arms, as great danger was apprehended by the citizens. The general supposition was that the Pottowattomies and Winnebagoes had joined Black Hawk, it being well known that his entire band, including women and children, that had gone up Rock river, did not exceed one thousand persons. Dwellings were vacated and most of the inhabitants repaired to the stockades for safety.

===Chinese===
溃逃者中有一名卫理公会传道人，他的马跑得太慢，甩不开印第安人，便想出一条别出心裁的计策来保全自己和马匹。行至一道冲沟，他离开追兵的踪迹一段距离，沿沟下行，找到一处足以遮蔽他和马匹的地方，在那里安然躲了两个钟头。他有心逐一数清前进而过的印第安人，守候他们回程；确信人已过尽、都已继续赶回营地去了，他才悄悄离开藏身之处，缓辔而行，次日拂晓时分到达迪克森渡口。

他报告了自己的做法和所用的计策——正是靠这些，他才在那批驱散大军、将之赶跑的印第安人面前保得平安。有人盘问印第安人的数目，他报称**二十五人**；这可激怒了几位比他早几小时回到营地、一口咬定敌数有一千五到两千的*「勇武」的*志愿兵！但许多志愿兵都熟知他，敬重他是位谦卑温和的基督徒绅士，便站在他一边，使他免遭人身伤害。

次日，即 5 月 15 日早晨约 8 点，这场惨败的消息传到加利纳，我们团立即奉令备战，市民们深感大祸将至。当时普遍的推测是波塔瓦托米族和温尼贝戈族已与黑鹰合流——众所周知，黑鹰溯罗克河而上的全部部众连同妇孺在内不过一千人上下。居民纷纷弃家，大多数人躲进围寨避难。

===Original===
The news of Stillman’s defeat “by 2,000 bloodthirsty Indian warriors” spread fast, far and wide, and the Governor of Illinois called for more volunteers; and when the news reached Washington, the Secretary of War ordered Gen. Scott, then at New York, to take a thousand soldiers and proceed to the seat of war and take command of the army.

This violation of a flag of truce, the wanton murder of its bearers, and the attack upon a mere remnant of Black Hawk’s band when sueing for peace, precipitated a war that should have been avoided.

[In confirmation of the dastardly act of the volunteers in killing the bearer of a white flag, and by which the war was precipitated, we give the following letter of Mr. Elijah Kilbourn, one of the scouts connected with Stillman’s command. Mr. K. is the man Black Hawk makes mention of in his narrative as having been taken captive during our last war with Great Britain, and by him adopted into the Sac tribe; and again taken prisoner by three of his braves at the battle of Sycamore creek.]

===Chinese===
斯蒂尔曼部「败于 2,000 名嗜血的印第安战士」的消息风驰电掣般传向四面八方，伊利诺伊州州长号召征募更多志愿兵；消息传到华盛顿，陆军部长即令当时驻纽约的斯科特将军（Gen. Scott）率一千士兵开赴战地，统率全军。

就这样，休战旗被悍然违犯，执旗者横遭杀害，黑鹰部众所余无几的残部在乞和之际又遭攻击——一场本可避免的战争，就此被猛然引发。

〔为证实志愿兵杀死白旗执旗者这一卑怯行径——战争正是由此而触发——我们转载斯蒂尔曼麾下侦察兵伊莱贾·基尔伯恩先生（Mr. Elijah Kilbourn）的一封信如下。这位基先生，就是黑鹰在自述中提到的那个人：上次我们对大不列颠的战争期间他曾被黑鹰俘获，并被黑鹰收养入索克族；其后在西卡莫尔溪之战中，又被黑鹰手下的三名勇士再次俘虏。〕

## Kilbourn’s Narrative A Reminiscence of Black Hawk / 基尔伯恩自述：对黑鹰的追忆

===Original===
From the Soldier’s Cabinet.

Much has been said both for and against the Indian character; but we doubt whether greater or nobler qualities have ever been exhibited in the conduct of civilized rulers or commanders than are shown in the incidents we are about to relate concerning Black Hawk, whose deeds upon the northwestern frontier will render his name illustrious while history exists.

Elijah Kilbourn, the subject of the great chieftain’s kindness, and to whom we are indebted for the present sketch, was a native of Pennsylvania. Just before the outbreak of the late war with Great Britain, he left the place of his birth to join the stirring scenes of adventure on the borders; and although now an old man, he still remembers, and loves to recount, the deed, and perils of his younger days, and especially those we are about to record.

===Chinese===
选自《军人文库》（Soldier’s Cabinet）。

关于印第安人的品性，赞誉与诋毁的言论都已听得多矣；然而我们怀疑，文明国度的统治者或统帅们的行止之中，是否展现过比我们行将讲述的这几件黑鹰逸事更为伟大、更为高贵的品质——他在西北边疆的功业，将使他的英名与史同存。

伊莱贾·基尔伯恩（Elijah Kilbourn）便是这位伟大酋长仁善之举的受惠者，我们这篇记事也得力于他的讲述。他是宾夕法尼亚人。就在上次对英战争爆发之前，他离开出生之地，投身边境上种种激动人心的历险；如今虽已届暮年，他仍记得——也乐于讲述——年轻时代的种种事迹与险境，尤其是我们即将记录的这些。

===Original===
“We had been,” commenced Kilbourn, in whose own language the story shall be given, “scouting through the country that lay about Fort Stephenson, when early one morning one of our number came in with the intelligence that the Fort was besieged by a combined force of British and Indians. We were very soon after in our saddles, bearing down with all speed in that direction for the express purpose of joining in the fight﻿—but on arriving, we found that the enemy had been signally repulsed by the brave little garrison under the command of Major Crogan. Our disappointment at learning this was, however, in a measure lessened, when we learned that Black Hawk, the leader of the savages, had, soon after the termination of the battle, gone with some twenty of his warriors back to his village on Rock river, whither we instantly determined to follow him.

“At sunrise the next morning we were on his trail, and followed it with great care to the banks of a stream. Here we ascertained that the savages had separated into nearly equal parties﻿—the one keeping straight down the banks of the stream, while the other had crossed to the other side and continued on toward Rock river. A council was now held, in which the oldest members of our party gave it as their opinion that Black Hawk had changed his intention of going to his village, and had, with the greater part of his followers, pursued his way down the stream, while the rest had been sent by him for some purpose to the town. In this opinion all coincided; but still our leader, who was a very shrewd man, had some doubts on his mind concerning the movements of the chief, and therefore, to make everything sure, he detailed four of us to follow the trail across the stream, while he with the rest, some seven or eight in number, immediately took the one down the bank.

“We soon after found ourselves alone and in the vicinity of Indian settlements, and we were therefore obliged to move with the utmost caution, which had the effect of rendering our progress extremely slow. During the course of the following morning we came across a great many different trails and by these we were so perplexed that we resolved to return to the main body; but from the signs we had already seen we knew that such a step would be attended with the greatest risk, and so it was at last decided that it would be far more safe for all hands to separate, and each man look out for himself. This resolve was no sooner made than it was put into execution, and a few minutes later found me alone in the great wilderness. I had often been so before, but never before had I been placed in a situation as dangerous as the present one, for now on all sides I was surrounded by foes, who would rejoice in the shedding of my blood. But still I was not gong to give up easily, and looking well to my weapons and redoubling my caution, I struck off at an angle from the course I had first chosen, why I hardly knew.

“I encountered nothing very formidable till some two hours before sunset, when, just as I emerged from a tangled thicket, I perceived an Indian on his knees at a clear, sparkling spring, from which he was slaking his thirst. Instinctively I placed my rifle to my shoulder, drew a bead upon the savage and pulled the trigger. Imagine, if you can, my feelings as the flint came down and was shivered to pieces while the priming remained unignited.

“The next moment the savage was up on his feet, his piece levelled directly at me and his finger pressing the trigger. There was no escape; I had left my horse in the woods some time before. The thicket behind me was too dense to permit me to enter it again quickly, and there was no tree within reach of sufficient size to protect me from the aim of my foe, who, now finding me at his mercy, advanced, his gun still in its threatening rest, and ordered me to surrender. Resistance and escape were alike out of the question, and I accordingly delivered myself up his prisoner, hoping by some means or other to escape at some future period. He now told me, in good English, to proceed in a certain direction. I obeyed him, and had not gone a stone’s throw before, just as I turned a thick clump of trees, I came suddenly upon an Indian camp, the one to which my captor undoubtedly belonged.

“As we came up all the savages, some six or eight in number, rose quickly and appeared much surprised at my appearing thus suddenly amongst their number; but they offered me no harm, and they behaved with most marked respect to my captor, whom, upon a close inspection, I recognized to be Black Hawk himself.

===Chinese===
「我们当时正在斯蒂芬森堡（Fort Stephenson）一带乡间执行侦察，」基尔伯恩如此开讲——下面的故事将用他本人的言语来讲述——「一天清早，我们中间的一人回来报信，说堡垒已被英国人与印第安人的联军围困。不多时我们便已上鞍，朝那个方向全速进发，专为赶去投入那场战斗——可一到那里，却发现敌人已被克罗甘少校（Major Crogan）麾下那支小小的英勇守军干净利落地击退。闻此消息，我们好生失望，不过随即又得知：野人的首领黑鹰在战斗结束后不久，已带着二十来名武士回他在罗克河边的村子去了。我们当即决定追踪前去。

「次日日出时分，我们踏上了他的踪迹，一路小心翼翼地追到一条溪流的岸边。在这里我们查明，野人已分成几乎相等的两伙——一伙沿溪岸径直而下，另一伙则渡到对岸，继续朝罗克河走去。于是我们开了一场议事，队里最年长的几位认为：黑鹰改变了回村子的主意，带着大部分随从沿溪而下了，其余的人则是他为某件事派往镇上的。众人都赞同这个看法；但我们的领队是个极为精明的人，对这位酋长的动向心里仍存着几分疑惑，因此为万全计，他分派我们四个去追渡过溪去的那条踪迹，自己带着其余七八个人立刻沿溪岸去追另一条。

「此后不久，我们便发觉自己已然孤身深入，而且逼近了印第安人的居留地，因此不得不格外小心地行动，结果行进得极为缓慢。第二天上午，我们碰到许许多多纵横交错的踪迹，被搅得晕头转向，便决意返回大队；可是从已经见到的种种迹象判断，我们知道这一步必伴着极大的风险，于是最后商定：众人分开各自照应自己，反倒最为稳妥。主意一定，立刻照办，几分钟后我便独自一人置身于茫茫荒野之中了。从前我也常孤身在外，却从未陷入过像眼下这样危险的境地，因为此刻我四面八方都是敌人，他们会为流我的血而欢欣。但我仍不肯轻易认输，仔细检视过武器，又加倍小心起来，然后偏离最初选定的路线，斜插出去——为什么这样，我自己也几乎说不清。

「直到日落前约莫两个钟头，我都没遇上什么可怕的东西。那时我刚从一片缠结的密林里钻出来，便看见一个印第安人跪在一泓清亮闪烁的泉水边饮水解渴。我本能地把步枪举上肩，对准那野人瞄了个准星，扣动了扳机。请你想象一下——倘若做得到——当燧石落下来摔得粉碎、火药却未被打燃的那一刻，我心里是什么滋味。

「转眼间那野人已一跃而起，枪口直指着我，手指压着扳机。逃是逃不掉的；我的马此前早已留在林中。身后的树丛太密，容不得我再迅速钻回去；近处又没有一棵够粗的大树，可以遮住我敌人的准头。他见我已在其掌握之中，便端着那管虎视眈眈的枪逼上前来，喝令我投降。抵抗与逃跑同样无望，我只好束手就擒，做了他的俘虏，指望日后设法脱身。这时他用一口流利的英语吩咐我朝某个方向走。我依言而行，走出去不过一箭之地，刚绕过一丛茂密的树木，便猛然撞见一座印第安营地——擒住我的人无疑正是这营地里的人。

「我们走近时，那六八个野人一齐迅速起身，对我这样突然出现在他们中间显得大为惊讶；但他们并未伤害我，而且对擒我的人极为恭敬。我细细一打量，才认出他不是别人，正是黑鹰本人。

===Original===
“ ‘The white mole digs deep, but Ma-ka-tai-me-she-kia-kiak (Black Hawk) flies high and can see far off,’ said the chieftain in a deep, gutteral tone, addressing me.

“He then related to his followers the occasion of my capture, and as he did so they glared on me fiercely and handled their weapons in a threatening manner, but at the conclusion of his remarks they appeared better pleased, although I was the recipient of many a passing frown. He now informed me that he had told his young men that they were to consider me a brother, as he was going to adopt me into the tribe.

“This was to me but little better than death itself, but there was no alternative and so I was obliged to submit, with the hope of making my escape at some future time. The annunciation of Black Hawk, moreover, caused me great astonishment, and after pondering the matter I was finally forced to set down as its cause one of those unaccountable whims to which the savage temperment is often subject.

“The next morning my captors forced me to go with them to their village on Rock river, where, after going through a tedious ceremony, I was dressed and painted, and thus turned from a white man into an Indian.

“For nearly three years ensuing it was my constant study to give my adopted brothers the slip, but during the whole of that time I was so carefully watched and guarded that I never found an opportunity to escape.

“However, it is a long lane that has no turning, and so it proves in my case. Pretending to be well satisfied with my new mode of life, I at last gained upon the confidence of the savages, and one day when their vigilance was considerably relaxed, I made my escape and returned in safety to my friends, who had mourned for me as dead.

===Chinese===
「『白鼹鼠掘地虽深，马-卡-泰-米-谢-基亚-基亚克（Ma-ka-tai-me-she-kia-kiak，即黑鹰）却飞得高、望得远。』酋长以低沉而粗嘎的嗓音，对着我如是说道。

「接着他向随从们讲了我被俘的经过；他讲的时候，他们恶狠狠地瞪着我，摆弄着兵器，杀气腾腾，可他话音落时，众人神色似乎和悦了些，尽管仍有不少皱眉蹙额瞥过我来。这时他告诉我，他已吩咐过族中的年轻人，要他们把我当作兄弟，因为他打算把我收养入族。

「这在我看来比死好不了多少，可是别无选择，只得屈从，只盼日后寻机逃脱。何况黑鹰这一宣告令我大感惊愕；我把这事思来想去，最后只能归结为野人脾性中常有的那种莫名难解的怪念头。

「第二天早晨，掳我的人强迫我随他们去罗克河边他们的村子。到了那里，经过一套冗长的仪式，我被换上装束、涂了油彩，就这样由白人变成了印第安人。

「此后将近三年，我无时无刻不在盘算怎样甩开我这群义兄弟；可整整那段时日里，我被看得太紧、守得太严，始终没找到脱身的机会。

「然而，路必有弯，天无绝人之路，我的际遇也证明了这一点。我装出对新生活心满意足的样子，终于渐渐赢得了野人的信任；有一天他们警戒大为松懈，我便趁机脱逃，平安回到亲友身边——他们早已把我当作死者哀悼过了。

===Original===
“Many years after this I was a participant in the battle at Sycamore Creek, which, as you know, is a tributary of Rock river. I was employed by the government as a scout, in which capacity it was acknowledged that I had no superior; but I felt no pride in hearing myself praised, for I knew I was working against Black Hawk, who, although he was an Indian, had once spared my life, and I was one never to forget a kindness. And besides this I had taken a great liking to him, for there was something noble and generous in his nature. However, my first duty was to my country, and I did my duty at all hazards.

“Now you must know that Black Hawk, after moving west of the Mississippi, had re-crossed, contrary to his agreement, not, however, from any hostile motive, but to raise a crop of corn and beans with the Pottowattomies and Winnebagoes, of which his own people stood in the utmost need. With this intention he had gone some distance up Rock river, when an express from General Atkinson ordered him peremptorily to return. This order the old chief refused to obey, saying that the General had no right to issue it. A second express from Atkinson threatened Black Hawk that if he did not return peaceably, force would be resorted to. The aged warrior became incensed at this and utterly refused to obey the mandate, but at the same time sent word to the General that he would not be the first one to commence hostilities.

“The movement of the renowned warrior was immediately trumpeted abroad as an invasion of the State, and with more rashness than wisdom, Governor Reynolds ordered the Illinois militia to take the field, and these were joined by the regulars, under General Atkinson, at Rock Island. Major Stillman, having under his command two hundred and seventy-five mounted men, the chief part of whom were volunteers, while a few like myself were regular scouts, obtained leave of General Whitesides, then lying at Dixon’s Ferry, to go on a scouting expedition.

“I knew well what would follow; but still, as I was under orders, I was obliged to obey, and together with the rest proceeded some thirty miles up Rock river to where Sycamore creek empties into it. This brought us to within six or eight miles of the camp of Black Hawk, who, on that day﻿—May 14th﻿—was engaged in preparing a dog feast for the purpose of fitly celebrating a contemplated visit of some Pottawattomie chiefs.

“Soon after preparing to camp we saw three Indians approach us bearing a white flag; and these, upon coming up, were made prisoners. A second deputation of five were pursued by some twenty of our mounted militia, and two of them killed, while the other three escaped. One of the party that bore the white flag was, out of the most cowardly vindictiveness, shot down while standing a prisoner in camp. The whole detachment, after these atrocities, now bore down upon the camp of Black Hawk, whose braves, with the exception of some forty or fifty, were away at a distance.

===Chinese===
「此后多年，我参加了西卡莫尔溪一役——你是知道的，那是罗克河的一条支流。我受政府雇用当侦察兵，众人公认干这一行无人胜过我；但听到人家夸我，我心里并不得意，因为我知道自己是在与黑鹰为敌。他虽是印第安人，却曾饶过我的性命，而我这个人，受了恩惠永不忘记。何况我本来就十分喜欢他，因为他的天性里有一种高贵豪爽的气度。然而，我对国家的义务高于一切，我便不顾一切地尽了本分。

「现在你该知道，黑鹰迁到密西西比河以西之后，又违约渡了回来——不过并非出于任何敌意，而是要同波塔瓦托米族与温尼贝戈族一起种一季玉米和豆子，他的族人正极度缺粮。他抱着这个打算沿罗克河上行了一段路程，阿特金森将军派来的信使严令他返回。老酋长拒绝奉命，说将军无权下这道命令。阿特金森的第二名信使又威胁黑鹰：若不和平退回，便要诉诸武力。老战士闻言大怒，断然抗命不遵，但同时也传话给将军：他绝不会先启战端。

「这位名震四方的战士的一举一动，立刻被宣扬成对伊利诺伊州的入侵。雷诺兹州长（Governor Reynolds）行事鲁莽多于明智，下令伊利诺伊民兵开赴战场；在罗克岛，这些民兵又与阿特金森将军麾下的正规军会合。斯蒂尔曼少校手下有二百七十五名乘马兵，其中大部分是志愿兵，只有少数像我这样的正规侦察兵；他向当时驻扎在迪克森渡口的怀特塞兹将军请得了许可，出发执行一次侦察任务。

「我很清楚接下来会发生什么；但我身为部下，奉命行事，只得从命，随众人沿罗克河上行约三十英里，到西卡莫尔溪汇入罗克河之处。这就把我们带到了距黑鹰营地只有六到八英里的地方。那一天——5 月 14 日——他正忙着筹备一场狗宴，以便得体地庆祝几位波塔瓦托米酋长即将到来的访问。

「刚刚安营，我们便看见三个印第安人举着白旗向我们走来；这些人一到近前，就被扣作了俘虏。第二批五人的使团被我方约二十名乘马民兵追击，两人被杀，三人逃脱。而举白旗的那队人中，有一人竟出于最卑怯的报复心，在身为俘虏、立于营中之时被枪杀。干下这些暴行之后，整支分遣队便扑向黑鹰的营地——那里除了四五十人外，勇士们大都远在别处。

===Original===
“As we rode up, a galling and destructive fire was poured in upon us by the savages, who, after discharging their guns, sprung from their coverts on either side, with their usual horrible yells, and continued the attack with their tomahawks and knives. My comrades fell around me like leaves; and happening to cast my eyes behind me, I beheld the whole detachment of militia flying from the field. Some four or five of us were left unsupported in the very midst of the foe, who, renewing their yells, rushed down upon us in a body. Gideon Munson and myself were taken prisoners, while others were instantly tomahawked and scalped. Munson, during the afternoon, seeing, as he supposed, a good opportunity to escape, recklessly attempted to do so, but was immediately shot down by his captor. And I now began to wish that they would serve me in the same manner, for I knew that if recognized by the savages, I should be put to death by the most horrible tortures. Nothing occurred, however, to give me any real uneasiness upon this point till the following morning, when Black Hawk, passing by me, turned and eyed me keenly for a moment or so. Then, stepping close to me, he said in a low tone: ‘*Does the mole think that Black Hawk forgets?*’

“Stepping away with a dignified air, he now left me, as you may well suppose, bordering in despair, for I knew too well the Indian character to imagine for a single instant that my life would be spared under the circumstances. I had been adopted into the tribe by Black Hawk, had lived nearly three years among them, and by escaping had incurred their displeasure, which could only be appeased with my blood. Added to this, I was now taken prisoner at the very time that the passions of the savages were most highly wrought upon by the mean and cowardly conduct of the whites. I therefore gave up all hope, and doggedly determined to meet stoically my fate.

“Although the Indians passed and repassed me many times during the day, often bestowing on me a buffet or a kick, yet not one of them seemed to remember me as having formerly been one of the tribe. At times this infused me with a faint hope, which was always immediately after extinguished, as I recalled to mind my recognition by Black Hawk himself.

“Some two hours before sunset Black Hawk again came to where I was bound, and having loosened the cords with which I was fastened to a tree, my arms still remaining confined, bade me follow him. I immediately obeyed him, not knowing what was to be my doom, though I expected none other than death by torture. In silence we left the encampment, not one of the savages interfering with us or offering me the slightest harm or indignity. For nearly an hour we strode on through the gloomy forest, now and then starting from its retreat some wild animal that fled upon our approach. Arriving at a bend of the river my guide halted, and turning toward the sun, which was rapidly setting, he said, after a short pause:

“ ‘I am going to send you back to your chief, though I ought to kill you for running away a long time ago, after I had adopted you as a son﻿—but Black Hawk can forgive as well as fight. When you return to your chief I want you to tell him all my words. Tell him that Black Hawk’s eyes have looked upon many suns, but they shall not see many more; and that his back is no longer straight, as in his youth, but is beginning to bend with age. The Great Spirit has whispered among the tree tops in the morning and evening and says that Black Hawk’s days are few, and that he is wanted in the spirit land. He is half dead, his arm shakes and is no longer strong, and his feet are slow on the war path. Tell him all this, and tell him, too,’ continued the untutored hero of the forest, with trembling emotion and marked emphasis, ‘that Black Hawk would have been a friend to the whites, but they would not let him, and that the hatchet was dug up by themselves and not by the Indians. Tell your chief that Black Hawk meant no harm to the palefaces when he came across the Mississippi, but came peaceably to raise corn for his starving women and children, and that even then he would have gone back, but when he sent his white flag the braves who carried it were treated like squaws and one of them inhumanly shot. Tell him too,’ he concluded with terrible force, while his eyes fairly flashed fire, ‘*that Black Hawk will have revenge*, and that he will never stop until the Great Spirit shall say to him, *come away*.’

“Thus saying he loosened the cord that bound my arms, and after giving me particular directions as to the best course to pursue to my own camp, bade me farewell and struck off into the trackless forest, to commence that final struggle which was decided against the Indians.

===Chinese===
「我们纵马逼近时，野人们一阵猛烈而毁灭性的火力向我们倾泻而来；他们放完枪，便带着惯常的可怖吼叫从两侧隐蔽处纵身跃出，用战斧和刀继续攻击。我的同袍们如落叶般纷纷倒在我四周；我偶然回头一望，只见整支民兵分队正溃逃出战场。我们四五个人失去接应，被撇在敌群正中央；敌人重新呐喊起来，成群向我们猛扑过来。吉迪恩·芒森（Gideon Munson）和我被生擒，其余的人当场被战斧劈死、剥了头皮。当天下午，芒森自以为瞅准了脱逃的好时机，冒失地动身欲逃，立时就被擒他的人开枪打死。这时我倒开始盼望他们同样处置我了，因为我知道，一旦被野人认出来，我将被处以最骇人的酷刑而死。不过直到次日早晨，才真有事情叫我为此惶恐不安：黑鹰从我身旁经过时，转过身来，锐利地盯视了我片刻，然后凑近我，低声说道：『*鼹鼠以为黑鹰忘记了吗？*』

「他仪态威严地踱开了，把我撇在那里——你可以想见——几近绝望；因为我对印第安人的品性知之太深，绝不敢有片刻妄想，以为自己在此情形下性命得以保全。我曾被黑鹰收养入族，在他们中间生活过将近三年，逃脱一事已触怒了他们，而非我之血不足以平息其怒。加之我又恰在野人的怒火被白人卑劣怯懦的行径煽至最炽之时被俘。于是我万念俱灰，横下心来，决意坚忍地迎接我的命运。

「那天里，印第安人从我面前来来去去走过许多趟，时不时赏我一记耳光或一脚，但似乎没有一个人记得我曾是族中一员。这有时给我注入一丝微弱的希望，可那希望总是随即熄灭，因为我记起黑鹰本人已经认出了我。

「日落前约两小时，黑鹰又来到我被捆绑的地方，解开把我拴在树上的绳索——双臂仍被缚着——吩咐我跟他走。我立刻依从，不知将有何等下场，虽然料想无非是受刑而死。我们默默离开营地，没有一个野人阻拦我们，也没有人对我有丝毫伤害或凌辱。将近一个钟头，我们大步穿过阴森的森林，不时惊起潜伏的野兽，在我们走近时仓皇逃去。到了河湾处，我的引路人停下脚步，朝着急速西沉的太阳转过身去，略顿了顿，说道：

「『我要放你回去见你的首领，虽然按理我该杀了你——很久以前你逃走时，我已收你为子——可黑鹰既会征战，也会宽恕。你回到首领那里，我要你把我的话一字不漏地转告他。告诉他：黑鹰的眼睛见过许多个太阳，但见不了几个了；他的脊背不再像年轻时那样挺直，已开始随年岁佝偻。大神清晨与黄昏在树梢间低语，说黑鹰的日子不多了，亡灵之国在等他前去。他已经半死：手臂发颤，不再有力，双脚踏上征途也迟缓了。把这一切都告诉他，还要告诉他，』这位未经教化的森林英雄接着说，声音激动颤抖、字字着力，『黑鹰本愿做白人的朋友，是他们不容他；战斧是他们自己挖出来的，不是印第安人。告诉你的首领：黑鹰渡过密西西比河时，对白面孔的人并无恶意，只是平平静静地来为挨饿的妇孺种玉米；即便到那时他也愿意回去，可他派出白旗时，举旗的勇士们竟被当作妇人一般对待，其中一个还惨遭枪杀。还要告诉他，』他结语时语气凛厉可畏，双眼几乎喷出火来，『*黑鹰定要复仇*，大神一日不对他说*来吧*，他一日不止。』

「说罢，他解开缚我双臂的绳索，又细细指点了我回本营的最佳路径，与我道了别，便拔脚走进无路可循的森林，去投入那场终局之战——一场以印第安人的败局告终的战争。

===Original===
“After the war was over, and the renowned Black Hawk had been taken prisoner, he was sent to Washington and the largest cities of the seaboard, that he might be convinced how utterly useless it was for him to contend against fate. It was enough, and the terrible warrior returned to the seclusion of his wilderness home, while the scepter of his chieftainship was given to the celebrated Keokuk.

“On the occasion of the ceremony by which Black Hawk was shorn of his power, and which took place on Rock Island, in the Mississippi, I shook the hand of the great chief, who appeared highly pleased to meet me once more; and upon parting with me he said with mournful dignity, as he cast above him a glance of seeming regret: ‘My children think I am too old to lead them any more!’

“This was the last time I ever saw him; and the next I learned of him was that he had left his old hunting grounds forever, and his spirit had gone to that bar where the balance will be rightly adjusted between the child of the forest and his paleface brethren.”

Although the Winnebagoes and the Pottowattomies had resolved to take no part in the war, a few young men from each of these tribes, being emboldened by Black Hawk’s victory in the engagement with Stillman’s regiment, concluded to join him. As the party moved up the river, war parties were sent out, in one of which the Winnebagoes joined, whilst the Pottowattomies, some twenty-five or thirty, went alone on the war path into a settlement that had been made on Indian creek, not far from its entrance into Fox river, and killed fifteen men, women and children, and took two young ladies prisoners, the Misses Hall, whom two young Sacs, who had just rode up, took upon their horses and carried them to a Winnebago camp, with a request that they be delivered to the whites. They were returned soon after, and to the writer said they had been well treated by the Winnebagoes.

On the 19th of June a message came into Galena from Kellogg’s Grove, with a report that a party of Indians had been seen in that neighborhood and that they had stolen some horses. Captain James Stephenson, with twelve picked men from his company, started immediately in pursuit of the Indians. On seeing him approach they took to the brush, when the Captain and his men dismounted. Leaving one to hold the horses, the balance entered the thicket, and two of them were killed at the first fire of the Indians, while three of the enemy were laid prostrate. For the purpose of reloading, Capt. Stephenson ordered a retreat, which was a bad move, as it gave the Indians time to reload and seek trees for safety. Capt. Stephenson1 and party again advanced, both parties firing simultaneously, each losing a man, when an Indian who had been secreted behind a tree rushed forward with his knife, but was suddenly checked by one of the soldiers running his bayonet through him. While in this position he seized the bayonet with both hands and had almost succeeded in pushing it out, when another soldier rushed forward, and with one stroke of his knife almost severed the head from his body. In this engagement Capt. S. lost three of the best men of his company and the Indians five, just one-half of their number.

On the return of Capt. Stephenson and party the news of his loss of three men, who were well known and highly respected, soon spread over town and caused much sorrow among their many friends. After learning the mode of attack, military men generally criticized it severely.

===Chinese===
「战争结束后，赫赫有名的黑鹰沦为俘虏，被送往华盛顿和沿海几座最大的城市，好让他信服：与命运抗争，对他来说是何等的徒劳。这就够了。这位可畏的战士回到了他荒野家园的幽居之中，而他酋长的权杖，则交到了有名的基奥卡克手里。

「在剥夺黑鹰权力的仪式上——仪式在密西西比河中的罗克岛举行——我握了这位伟大酋长的手；他显得极为高兴与我重逢。与我作别时，他抬眼向上投去一瞥，似有憾意，以凄然而庄重的语气说道：『我的孩子们觉得我太老，不能再带领他们了！』

「这是我最后一次见到他。此后我听到关于他的消息是：他已永远离开了古老的猎场，他的魂灵已去往那座天平之前——在那里，森林之子与他白面孔的兄弟之间的是非恩怨，终将得到公正的衡量。」

温尼贝戈族和波塔瓦托米族虽然决议不参与这场战争，两族中各有一些年轻人被黑鹰对斯蒂尔曼团一战的胜利所鼓舞，决意投到他麾下。队伍沿河上行时，数支战队被派了出去，其中一支有温尼贝戈人参加；而波塔瓦托米人约有二十五到三十名，独自踏上征途，攻入建在印第安溪边的一处移民定居点——那里距印第安溪注入福克斯河（Fox river）之处不远——杀死了十五名男女和孩童，还掳走了两位年轻的小姐，霍尔家姐妹（the Misses Hall）。恰在此时，两名索克族青年骑马赶到，把姐妹俩载上马背，送到一座温尼贝戈营地，请求把她们交还白人。不久两人即被送回，并对本文作者说，温尼贝戈人对她们礼遇有加。

6 月 19 日，一封急报从凯洛格林地（Kellogg's Grove）送到加利纳，报告说有人在那一带看见一队印第安人，还偷走了几匹马。詹姆斯·斯蒂文森上尉（Captain James Stephenson）率连中十二名精选士兵立即出动追击。印第安人望见他逼近，窜入了灌木丛，上尉和部下随即下马。留一人看马，其余的人进入树丛；印第安人的第一轮排枪就打死我方两人，敌方也有三人中弹倒地。为便于重新装弹，斯蒂文森上尉下令后撤——这是一步败着，因为它给了印第安人重新装弹、寻树掩蔽的时机。斯蒂文森上尉率队再度进逼，双方同时开火，各折一人；此时一名藏在树后的印第安人持刀猛冲上来，却被一名士兵一刺刀戳穿，骤然遏住。他就势双手攥住刺刀，几乎就要把它拔了出去，另一名士兵抢步上前，一刀险些把他的头颅割了下来。此役斯蒂文森上尉折了连中三名最优秀的士兵，印第安人损失五人，恰是其人数的一半。

斯蒂文森上尉一行归来后，三名阵亡者——他们都颇有名望、深受敬重——的死讯很快传遍镇上，在众多亲友中引起深切的悲痛。军界人士了解了进攻的方式之后，大多严词批评。

##### Battle of Pecatonica / 佩卡托尼卡之战

===Original===
On the 14th of June, a mall scouting party of Sacs killed five men at the Spafford farm, and on reception of the news next day, Gen. Atkinson ordered Col. Henry Dodge to take command of Posey’s brigade, then stationed near Fort Hamilton, and while on his way from Fort Union, where his regiment was in camp, to visit the brigade, he heard the sharp crack of a rifle, and instantly looking in the direction of the sound, saw a man fall from his horse, who had been shot by Indians nearby. Instead of going forward as he set out to do, he hastily returned to his command, mustered a portion of his cavalry and went in pursuit of the Indians, and soon got on the trail of twenty-five warriors, who had commenced their retreat soon after shooting, and espying him, hastened back to the front. The Indians crossed and re-crossed the Pecatonica river several times, being closely pushed by Col. Dodge and his men, and finding escape hopeless, made a stand. The colonel immediately dismounted his men and picked his way cautiously, with the intention of firing and then charging upon them. But the Indians, being on the lookout, watched their opportunity and got the first fire, by which a brave soldier named Apple was killed, and another by the name of Jenkins was wounded. The fight continued vigorously until the last Indian was killed, several of them having been shot while trying to escape by swimming. At the commencement of the fight, the forces on each side were nearly equal, but the Indians, in swimming the river, had got their powder wet, and although they made desperate efforts to close in on our men with knives, they were shot down in their endeavors.

Col. Dodge, in speaking of this engagement, at Galena, after the close of the war, said he was amazed at the desperation displayed by a big, burly brave, who came towards him with gun at his shoulder and halted quickly when only a few paces from him, drew the trigger, and was sorely disappointed in his gun not going off. Quick as thought the colonel brought his rifle in position, pulled the trigger, but, owing to the dampness of the powder, it failed to go off. In the meantime the brave was coming towards him, knife in hand and desperation in his eye, and when only a few feet from him the colonel shot him down with his revolver. At the same time one of his brave boys, by the name of Beach, was engaged in a desperate encounter with the last remaining savage, in which both used knives; the Indian was killed and Beach very badly wounded.

Thus ended one of the most sanguinary engagements of the war.

===Chinese===
6 月 14 日，一小队索克族侦察兵在斯帕福德农场（Spafford farm）杀了五个人。次日消息传来，阿特金森将军命令亨利·道奇上校（Col. Henry Dodge）接管当时驻扎在汉密尔顿堡（Fort Hamilton）附近的波西旅。道奇原从其团部扎营的尤宁堡（Fort Union）动身前去视察该旅，途中忽闻一声清脆的枪响，循声望去，只见一个人中弹坠马——开枪的是近旁的印第安人。他没有按原计划继续赶路，而是急返部队，集结一部分骑兵，出发追击印第安人，很快便咬上了二十五名武士的踪迹；这些人开枪后不久便已回撤，此时望见道奇，又急忙折回前面去。印第安人在佩卡托尼卡河（Pecatonica river）上来回渡了几次，被道奇上校的人紧迫不舍，眼见脱逃无望，便停下应战。上校当即令部下下马，小心翼翼地择路而进，打算先放枪再冲锋。但印第安人早有防备，瞅准时机抢了先手：一名叫阿普尔（Apple）的勇敢士兵阵亡，另一名叫詹金斯（Jenkins）的负了伤。战斗激烈进行，直到最后一个印第安人被击毙为止，其中有几人是泅水逃命时被打死的。交战之初，双方兵力几乎相当，但印第安人泅渡时火药浸了水，尽管他们拼死想持刀逼近我方人员，却在努力之中纷纷中弹倒下。

道奇上校战后在加利纳谈到此役时说，有一个身材魁梧的勇士，那股亡命的狠劲令他惊叹：那人端着枪向他逼来，距他只剩几步时猛然站定，扣动扳机，却因枪没有响而大失所望。说时迟那时快，上校端起步枪扣动扳机，却也因为火药受潮，枪未能击发。与此同时，那勇士手握尖刀、眼中凶光毕露地向他扑来；相距只有几英尺时，上校用手枪把他击倒在地。同一时刻，他麾下一名叫比奇（Beach）的勇敢小伙子，正与最后一个野人殊死相搏，两人都用刀；印第安人被杀，比奇也身负重伤。

这场战争中最血腥的战斗之一，就此告终。

##### Fight at Apple River Fort / 苹果河堡之战

===Original===
Capt. A. W. Snyder’s Company, of Col. Henry’s Regiment, was detailed to guard the country between Galena and Fox and Rock rivers, and was surprised on the night of the 17th of June, while encamped in the vicinity of Burr Oak Grove. His sentinels, while on duty, were fired upon by Indians, who did not deem it prudent to continue the attack, but immediately fled. As soon as it was light enough next morning to follow their trail, Capt. Snyder started with his company, but on reaching their camp, found that they had fled on his approach. He redoubled his speed and continued on their trail until he overtook them. Finding that there was no escape, the Indians got into a deep gully for protection, but were soon surrounded, when Capt. Snyder ordered his men to charge upon them.
